| [[Tigalari script|Tigalari]]{{efn|A [[Brahmic scripts|Southern Brahmic]] script which was primarily used for writing [[Vedas|Vedic texts]] in ''Sanskrit''. Presently used in the [[Tulu Nadu]] region by [[Brahmin|Brahmin communities]] (''Shivalli'', ''Havyaka'', & ''Kota'') to document Vedic mantras, [[Upanishads]], [[Hindu astrology|Jyotisha]] and [[Dharmaśāstra]].}}<br />({{lang|sa-Tutg|𑎱𑏌𑎱𑏎𑎒𑎽𑎡𑏌}})<br />{{small|(''Used in [[Tulu Nadu|Tulu Nadu region]] in a religious-liturgical context.'')}}
| (''being encoded'')

| [[Tirhuta script|Tirhuta]]{{efn|Originating in the [[Mithila (region)|Mithila region]] (modern-day [[Bihar]] and parts of [[Nepal]]), ''Tirhuta'' was the primary vehicle for ''Sanskrit'' in [[East India|Eastern India]] for centuries. It was used for extensive Sanskrit works, like [[Panjis|''Panjikas'']] (genealogical records), religious texts, and literature by Maithil Pundits.}}<br />({{lang|sa-Tirh|𑒮𑓀𑒮𑓂𑒏𑒵𑒞𑒧𑓂}})<br />{{small|(''Used in [[East India|Eastern India]] in a religious-liturgical context'')}}
| {{lang|sa-Tirh|𑒁𑒢𑒳𑒔𑓂𑒕𑒹𑒠𑓁 𑓑 - 𑒮𑒩𑓂𑒫𑒹 𑒧𑒰𑒢𑒫𑒰𑓁 𑒮𑓂𑒫𑒞𑒢𑓂𑒞𑓂𑒩𑒞𑒰𑓁 𑒮𑒧𑒳𑒞𑓂𑒣𑒢𑓂𑒢𑒰𑓁 𑒫𑒩𑓂𑒞𑒢𑓂𑒞𑒹 𑒁𑒣𑒱 𑒔, 𑒑𑒾𑒩𑒫𑒠𑒵𑒬𑒰 𑒁𑒡𑒱𑒏𑒰𑒩𑒠𑒵𑒬𑒰 𑒔 𑒮𑒧𑒰𑒢𑒰𑓁 𑒋𑒫 𑒫𑒩𑓂𑒞𑒢𑓂𑒞𑒹। 𑒋𑒞𑒹 𑒮𑒩𑓂𑒫𑒹 𑒔𑒹𑒞𑒢𑒰-𑒞𑒩𑓂𑒏-𑒬𑒏𑓂𑒞𑒱𑒦𑓂𑒨𑒰𑓀 𑒮𑒳𑒮𑒧𑓂𑒣𑒢𑓂𑒢𑒰𑓁 𑒮𑒢𑓂𑒞𑒱। 𑒁𑒣𑒱 𑒔, 𑒮𑒩𑓂𑒫𑒹𑓄𑒣𑒱 𑒥𑒢𑓂𑒡𑒳𑒞𑓂𑒫-𑒦𑒰𑒫𑒢𑒨𑒰 𑒣𑒩𑒮𑓂𑒣𑒩𑓀 𑒫𑓂𑒨𑒫𑒯𑒩𑒢𑓂𑒞𑒳 ॥}}

| [[Nandinagari]]{{efn|''Nandināgarī'' is a southern variant of the ''Nāgarī'' script, and it was the official script of the [[Vijayanagara Empire]]. Used extensively for Sanskrit copper plate inscriptions and palm-leaf manuscripts in [[Karnataka]], [[Andhra Pradesh]], and [[Maharashtra]]. ''Nandināgarī'' and ''Devanāgarī'' scripts are very close and share many similarities, but they also show systematic differences.}}<br />({{lang|sa-Nand|𑧍𑧞𑧍𑧠𑦮𑧖𑦽𑧆𑧠}})<br />{{small|(''Historical in [[Deccan Plateau|Deccan]] region. Presently inactive'')}}
| {{lang|sa-Nand|𑦠𑧁𑧔𑦳𑧠𑦴𑧚𑦿𑧟 ೧ - 𑧍𑧈𑧠𑧊𑧚 𑧆𑧑𑧁𑧊𑧑𑧟 𑧍𑧠𑧊𑦽𑧁𑧠𑦽𑧠𑧈𑦽𑧑𑧟 𑧍𑧆𑧔𑦽𑧠𑧂𑧁𑧠𑧁𑧑𑧟 𑧊𑧈𑧠𑦽𑧁𑧠𑦽𑧚 𑦠𑧂𑧒 𑦳, 𑦰𑧝𑧈𑧊𑦿𑧖𑧋𑧑 𑦠𑧀𑧒𑦮𑧑𑧈𑦿𑧖𑧋𑧑 𑦳 𑧍𑧆𑧑𑧁𑧑𑧟 𑦪𑧊 𑧊𑧈𑧠𑦽𑧁𑧠𑦽𑧚। 𑦪𑦽𑧚 𑧍𑧈𑧠𑧊𑧚 𑦳𑧚𑦽𑧁𑧑-𑦽𑧈𑧠𑦮-𑧋𑦮𑧠𑦽𑧒𑧅𑧠𑧇𑧑𑧞 𑧍𑧔𑧍𑧆𑧠𑧂𑧁𑧠𑧁𑧑𑧟 𑧍𑧁𑧠𑦽𑧒। 𑦠𑧂𑧒 𑦳, 𑧍𑧈𑧠𑧊𑧚𑧡𑧂𑧒 𑧄𑧁𑧠𑧀𑧔𑦽𑧠𑧊-𑧅𑧑𑧊𑧁𑧇𑧑 𑧂𑧈𑧍𑧠𑧂𑧈𑧞 𑧊𑧠𑧇𑧊𑧎𑧈𑧁𑧠𑦽𑧔 ॥}}

| [[Siddham script|Siddhaṃ]]{{efn|''Siddhaṃ'' means "''accomplished''", "''completed''" or "''perfected''" in Sanskrit. It was used in India from the 6th-13th centuries by [[History of Buddhism in India|Indian Buddhists]]. Its usage survives into the modern period for liturgical purposes in [[Japan]] and [[Korea]] by [[East Asian Buddhism|East Asian Buddhists]].}}<br />({{lang|sa-Sidd|𑖭𑖽𑖭𑖿𑖎𑖴𑖝𑖦𑖿}})<br />{{small|(''Historical in India, presently active in [[Japan]] & [[Korea]]'')}}
| {{lang|sa-Sidd|𑖀𑖡𑖲𑖓𑖿𑖔𑖸𑖟𑖾 १ - 𑖭𑖨𑖿𑖪𑖸 𑖦𑖯𑖡𑖪𑖯𑖾 𑖭𑖿𑖪𑖝𑖡𑖿𑖝𑖿𑖨𑖝𑖯𑖾 𑖭𑖦𑗜𑖝𑖿𑖢𑖡𑖿𑖡𑖯𑖾 𑖪𑖨𑖿𑖝𑖡𑖿𑖝𑖸 𑖀𑖢𑖰 𑖓, 𑖐𑖻𑖨𑖪𑖟𑖴𑖫𑖯 𑖀𑖠𑖰𑖎𑖯𑖨𑖟𑖴𑖫𑖯 𑖓 𑖭𑖦𑖯𑖡𑖯𑖾 𑖊𑖪 𑖪𑖨𑖿𑖝𑖡𑖿𑖝𑖸𑗂 𑖊𑖝𑖸 𑖭𑖨𑖿𑖪𑖸 𑖓𑖸𑖝𑖡𑖯-𑖝𑖨𑖿𑖎-𑖫𑖎𑖿𑖝𑖰𑖥𑖿𑖧𑖯𑖽 𑖭𑗜𑖭𑖦𑖿𑖢𑖡𑖿𑖡𑖯𑖾 𑖭𑖡𑖿𑖝𑖰𑗂 𑗅 𑖀𑖢𑖰 𑖓, 𑖭𑖨𑖿𑖪𑖸𑖢𑖰 𑖤𑖡𑖿𑖠𑗜𑖝𑖿𑖪-𑖥𑖯𑖪𑖡𑖧𑖯 𑖢𑖨𑖭𑖿𑖢𑖨𑖽 𑖪𑖿𑖧𑖪𑖮𑖨𑖡𑖿𑖝𑗜𑗂 𑗉}}{{efn|''Siddhaṃ'' is still mostly handwritten. Over time, additional markings were developed like punctuation marks, head marks, repetition marks, end marks, special ligatures and several ornaments of the scribe's choice, which are not currently encoded. The ''nuqta'' (𑗀 ) is also used in some modern ''Siddhaṃ'' texts.}}<ref>{{cite report |last=Pandey |first=Anshuman |date=1 August 2012 |title=N4294: Proposal to Encode the Siddham Script in ISO/IEC 10646 |url=https://www.unicode.org/L2/L2012/12234r-n4294-siddham.pdf |publisher=ISO/IEC JTC1/SC2/WG2 |type=Working Group Document |pages=5–13|access-date=23 January 2026}}</ref>

| [[Bhaiksuki script|Bhaiksuki]]{{efn|Bhaikshuki was used around the 11th-12th centuries in [[East India|Eastern India]] and parts of [[Tibet]] and [[Nepal]]. Very few manuscripts and attestations are available so far.}}<br />({{lang|sa-Bhks|𑰭𑰽𑰭𑰿𑰎𑰴𑰝𑰦𑰿}})<br />(''Ancient'')
| {{lang|sa-Bhks|𑰀𑰡𑰲𑰓𑰿𑰔𑰸𑰟𑰾 𑱑 - 𑰭𑰨𑰿𑰪𑰸 𑰦𑰯𑰡𑰪𑰯𑰾 𑰕𑰡𑰿𑰦𑰡𑰯𑰾 𑰭𑰿𑰪𑰝𑰡𑰿𑰝𑰿𑰨𑰯𑰾 𑰪𑰹𑰧𑰎𑰿𑰝𑰰𑰎𑰐𑰹𑰨𑰪𑰸𑰜 𑰀𑰠𑰰𑰎𑰯𑰨𑰸𑰜 𑰓 𑰝𑰲𑰩𑰿𑰧𑰯𑰾 𑰊𑰪 𑱁 𑰭𑰨𑰿𑰪𑰸𑰬𑰯𑰽 𑰪𑰰𑰪𑰸𑰎𑰾 𑰁𑰝𑰿𑰦𑰭𑰯𑰎𑰿𑰬𑰱 𑰓 𑰪𑰨𑰿𑰝𑰝𑰸 𑱁 𑰭𑰨𑰿𑰪𑰸 𑰢𑰨𑰭𑰿𑰢𑰨𑰽 𑰥𑰿𑰨𑰯𑰝𑰴𑰥𑰯𑰪𑰸𑰡 𑰪𑰿𑰧𑰪𑰮𑰨𑰸𑰧𑰲𑰾 𑱂}}

| [[Kharosthi|Kharoṣṭhī]]{{efn|''Kharoṣṭhī'' emerged in the [[Gandhara |Gandhara region]]. While it was primarily used for [[Gandhari language|''Gandhari Prakrit'']], it was also used to write ''Sanskrit'' in the northwestern areas and [[Central Asia]] until approximately the 4th century. It died out by the 7th century without leaving any modern descendant scripts.}}<br />({{lang|sa-Khar|𐨯𐨎𐨯𐨿𐨐𐨃𐨟𐨨𐨿}})<br />(''Archaic'')
| {{Rtl-para|sa-Khar|𐨀𐨣𐨂𐨕𐨿𐨖𐨅𐨡𐨏 𐩀 - 𐨯𐨪𐨿𐨬𐨅 𐨨𐨌𐨣𐨬𐨌𐨏 𐨯𐨿𐨬𐨟𐨣𐨿𐨟𐨿𐨪𐨟𐨌𐨏 𐨯𐨨𐨂𐨟𐨿𐨤𐨣𐨿𐨣𐨌𐨏 𐨬𐨪𐨿𐨟𐨣𐨿𐨟𐨅 𐨀𐨤𐨁 𐨕, 𐨒𐨆𐨌𐨪𐨬𐨡𐨃𐨭𐨌 𐨀𐨢𐨁𐨐𐨌𐨪𐨡𐨃𐨭𐨌 𐨕 𐨯𐨨𐨌𐨣𐨌𐨏 𐨀𐨅𐨬 𐨬𐨪𐨿𐨟𐨣𐨿𐨟𐨅𐩖 𐨀𐨅𐨟𐨅 𐨯𐨪𐨿𐨬𐨅 𐨕𐨅𐨟𐨣𐨌-𐨟𐨪𐨿𐨐-𐨭𐨐𐨿𐨟𐨁𐨧𐨿𐨩𐨌𐨎 𐨯𐨂𐨯𐨨𐨿𐨤𐨣𐨿𐨣𐨌𐨏 𐨯𐨣𐨿𐨟𐨁𐩖 𐨀𐨤𐨁 𐨕, 𐨯𐨪𐨿𐨬𐨅𐨤𐨁 𐨦𐨣𐨿𐨢𐨂𐨟𐨿𐨬-𐨧𐨌𐨬𐨣𐨩𐨌 𐨤𐨪𐨯𐨿𐨤𐨪𐨎 𐨬𐨿𐨩𐨬𐨱𐨪𐨣𐨿𐨟𐨂 𐩗}}{{efn|Unlike most Indian scripts, ''Kharoṣṭhī'' was written from right to left.}}
|-
| [[Brahmi script|Ashokan Brāhmī]]{{efn|''Brāhmī'' is the mother of almost all modern Indian scripts. It was used to record the earliest surviving Sanskrit inscriptions, such as the [[Junagadh rock inscription of Rudradaman]] (''c. 150 CE''). It developed into the [[Gupta script]], which then branched into northern scripts like ''Devanāgarī'' and ''Śāradā''.}}<br />({{lang|sa-Brah|𑀲𑀁𑀲𑁆𑀓𑀾𑀢𑀫𑁆}})<br />(''Archaic'')
| {{lang|sa-Brah|𑀅𑀦𑀼𑀘𑁆𑀙𑁂𑀤𑀂 𑁧 - 𑀲𑀭𑁆𑀯𑁂 𑀫𑀸𑀦𑀯𑀸𑀂 𑀲𑁆𑀯𑀢𑀦𑁆𑀢𑁆𑀭𑀢𑀸𑀂 𑀲𑀫𑀼𑀢𑁆𑀧𑀦𑁆𑀦𑀸𑀂 𑀯𑀭𑁆𑀢𑀦𑁆𑀢𑁂 𑀅𑀧𑀺 𑀘, 𑀕𑁅𑀭𑀯𑀤𑀾𑀰𑀸 𑀅𑀥𑀺𑀓𑀸𑀭𑀤𑀾𑀰𑀸 𑀘 𑀲𑀫𑀸𑀦𑀸𑀂 𑀏𑀯 𑀯𑀭𑁆𑀢𑀦𑁆𑀢𑁂 𑁇 𑀏𑀢𑁂 𑀲𑀭𑁆𑀯𑁂 𑀘𑁂𑀢𑀦𑀸-𑀢𑀭𑁆𑀓-𑀰𑀓𑁆𑀢𑀺𑀪𑁆𑀬𑀸𑀁 𑀲𑀼𑀲𑀫𑁆𑀧𑀦𑁆𑀦𑀸𑀂 𑀲𑀦𑁆𑀢𑀺𑁇 𑀅𑀧𑀺 𑀘, 𑀲𑀭𑁆𑀯𑁂𑀧𑀺 𑀩𑀦𑁆𑀥𑀼𑀢𑁆𑀯-𑀪𑀸𑀯𑀦𑀬𑀸 𑀧𑀭𑀲𑁆𑀧𑀭𑀁 𑀯𑁆𑀬𑀯𑀳𑀭𑀦𑁆𑀢𑀼 𑁈}}
